B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

高致病性禽流感在博恩县被检测到

Highly pathogenic avian influenza detected in Boone County
新闻简报On September 22, 2026, the Iowa Department of Agriculture and Land Stewardship confirmed a case of highly pathogenic avian influenza in Boone County, marking the state's seventh detection of the bird flu in 2026. The outbreak, which began in February 2022, has affected over 30 million birds in Iowa and more than 208 million birds nationally. The affected birds were part of a backyard, mixed species flock of 138. The agency urged poultry owners to enhance biosecurity measures and consult their veterinarians if they suspect illness. No human infections were reported, and the focus remains on preventing further spread among poultry. The situation is under active investigation, with no additional details provided on the specific strain or potential human risk.
查看新闻五要素
时间
爱荷华州农业与土地管理部于2026年9月22日确认了该病例。当前的暴发疫情始于2022年2月。
地点与对象
美国爱荷华州博恩县。受影响的人群包括当地家养的鸡和其他鸟类。
事件
爱荷华州农业与土地管理部确认在博恩县发现了一例高致病性禽流感,这是该州2026年第七次检测到禽流感。
规模、影响与风险
This is the seventh case of highly pathogenic avian influenza in Iowa in 2026. The outbreak has impacted more than 30 million birds in Iowa and over 208 million birds nationally since February 2022. The risk is explicitly stated as a concern for poultry owners and the broader agricultural sector.
应对、状态与不确定性
爱荷华州农业与土地管理部敦促家禽饲养者加强生物安全措施,并在怀疑鸟类患病时联系兽医。调查仍在进行中,未提供进一步的详细信息。

View five news elements
Time
The Iowa Department of Agriculture and Land Stewardship confirmed the case on September 22, 2026. The current outbreak started in February 2022.
Location and population
Boone County, Iowa, United States. The affected population includes backyard chickens and other birds in the area.
Event
A case of highly pathogenic avian influenza was confirmed in Boone County, Iowa, marking the state's seventh detection of the bird flu in 2026. The Iowa Department of Agriculture and Land Stewardship reported this.
Scale, impact and risk
This is the seventh case of highly pathogenic avian influenza in Iowa in 2026. The outbreak has impacted more than 30 million birds in Iowa and over 208 million birds nationally since February 2022. The risk is explicitly stated as a concern for poultry owners and the broader agricultural sector.
Response, status and uncertainty
The Iowa Department of Agriculture and Land Stewardship urged poultry owners to bolster biosecurity practices and contact their veterinarians if they suspect birds are sick. The investigation is ongoing, and no further details are provided.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

凯库拉的小企鹅被移出禽流感疫苗名单 - 1news.co.nz

Kaikōura’s little penguins left off bird flu vaccine list - 1news.co.nz
新闻简报文章报道,新西兰的环境保护部(DOC)已将小企鹅(kororā)从其禽流感疫苗接种名单中移除,理由是它们不面临灭绝风险,且疫苗接种对鸟类来说是劳动密集型且具有压力的。凯库拉野生动物中心信托基金和当地理事会对该决定表示失望,认为该物种数量少、正在减少,并面临捕食、人类干扰和海洋环境变化等重大威胁。DOC优先考虑像kākāpō和takahē这样的疫苗接种物种,这些物种濒临更严重的灭绝风险。信托基金和志愿者正通过紧急和预防性护理积极保护这些企鹅。DOC的疫苗接种计划专注于高度受威胁的物种,而信托基金认为疫苗接种可以以最小的干扰进行管理。文章强调需要采取更全面的物种保护方法,考虑除灭绝风险之外的多种威胁。
查看新闻五要素
时间
凯库拉副市长维琪·古勒福德表示,理事会对DOC的决定感到失望,因为凯库拉的小企鹅面临局部灭绝的风险。
地点与对象
新西兰凯库拉;小企鹅(kororā)具有数量少且正在减少的种群。
事件
凯库拉的小企鹅被新西兰政府的禽流感疫苗接种名单排除在DOC的保护计划之外,因为这些鸟类不被认为面临灭绝风险,且野生动物疫苗接种被认为对鸟类来说是劳动密集型且具有压力的。
规模、影响与风险
小企鹅被排除在疫苗接种计划之外的原因是其种群状况以及DOC对面临灭绝风险最严重的物种的优先考虑。文章提到这些鸟类面临局部灭绝的风险,繁殖地点有限,且需要大量干预护理。
应对、状态与不确定性
凯库拉野生动物中心信托基金和当地理事会呼吁将小企鹅纳入疫苗接种计划,但DOC尚未批准。信托基金和志愿者正通过紧急护理、预防性护理和植入芯片等方式努力保护该种群。DOC的优先级系统受到批评,认为其未纳入多威胁评估。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Kaikōura’s little penguins left off bird flu vaccine list - 1news.co.nz

News Brief
The article reports that the Department of Conservation (DOC) in New Zealand has excluded little penguins (kororā) from its bird flu vaccination list, citing that they are not at risk of extinction and that vaccinations are labor-intensive and stressful for the birds. The Kaikōura Wildlife Centre Trust and local councils have expressed disappointment with this decision, arguing that the population is small, declining, and faces significant threats such as predation, human disturbance, and changing marine conditions. DOC has prioritized species like kākāpō and takahē for vaccination, which are more critically endangered. The trust and volunteers are actively involved in protecting the penguins through emergency and preventative care. DOC’s vaccination programme focuses on highly threatened species, and the trust believes that vaccination could be managed with minimal distress. The article highlights the need for a more comprehensive approach to species protection that considers multiple threats beyond just extinction risk.
Original source text (English)
Top Stories Latest New Zealand World Sport Overview Rugby League Cricket Football Netball Basketball Other Sport Golf Motorsport Tennis Your Vote 2026 Life Entertainment Business Te Ao Māori Politics Newsletters ADVERTISEMENT New Zealand Local Democracy Reporting Kaikōura’s little penguins left off bird flu vaccine list Mon, Sep 21 An adult little penguin protects its chicks (Source: Local Democracy Reporting) SHARE ME Kaikōura wildlife advocates are disappointed the district’s little penguins have been left off the Government’s avian flu vaccination list. By David Hill, Local Democracy Reporter The Kaikōura Wildlife Centre Trust and local councils have called for the little penguin / kororā to be included in the Department of Conservation’s (DOC) H5N1 vaccination programme. But DOC said the little penguin was not included as the birds are not at risk of extinction and wildlife vaccinations were labour intensive and stressful on the birds. Kaikōura Deputy Mayor Vicki Gulleford said the council was disappointed at DOC’s decision, as Kaikōura’s little penguins were at risk of local extinction. ‘‘We have a really small population which our community and volunteers are trying to keep safe and trying to grow the numbers,” Gulleford said. ‘‘The birds are used to being handled so we firmly believe we could manage the vaccination without too much distress, and we would appreciate the Government looking at it further and supporting us.’’ Mayor Craig Mackle wrote to Conservation Minister Tama Potaka earlier this month backing the trust’s call for penguin vaccinations. Kaikōura Wildlife Centre Trust manager Sabrina Luecht said Kaikōura’s kororā had low and declining numbers, limited breeding locations and high intervention care. ‘‘DOC’s species prioritisation system is flawed and limited to threat classification only, failing to incorporate multi-threat assessments for highly threatened populations already at risk of extinction.’’ DOC rolls out bird flu vaccinations to five of NZ’s most threatened species As a highly infectious strain of H5N1 bird flu spreads around the world, natives such as the kākapō and takahē are being protected. DOC rolls out bird flu vaccinations to five of NZ’s most threatened species 2:10 As a highly infectious strain of H5N1 bird flu spreads around the world, natives such as the kākapō and takahē are being protected. (Source: 1News) Work to protect kororā was largely done by volunteers, who were permitted to provide emergency care, preventative care and microchipping for monitoring. It meant DOC did not have the same understanding about population numbers, she said. Environment Canterbury chairperson Dr Deon Swiggs said kororā were facing ‘‘significant pressures’’, including predation, human disturbance and changing marine conditions. ‘‘Kororā are an important part of Kaikōura's coastal ecosystem, and their long-term protection will require ongoing collaboration between community groups, councils, DOC and other partners.’’ Swiggs and councillor Genevieve Robinson wrote to Biosecurity Minister Andrew Hoggard earlier this month seeking support for localised responses to the impacts of avian influenza on native wildlife populations. Kororā/little penguin. (Source: Department of Conservation) ECan provided $25,000 this year to support kororā protection work, including a predator exclusion fence around a key nesting colony, and supporting the Kaikōura Wildlife Centre Trust’s initiatives. DOC H5 bird flu manager Ash Murphy said vaccination was only approved for emergency use for New Zealand's most threatened birds. ‘‘Each bird needs to be caught and identified, given a health check and swabbed, then vaccinated. ‘‘A second or booster dose is given three to four weeks after the first to provide full protection for up to six months.’’ Murphy said the Kaikōura Wildlife Centre Trust and other community groups played a valuable role in protecting kororā. Vaccination was not the best tool for most native species, which will need to develop immunity to survive, he said. Overseas experience had shown bird populations recovered as they developed immunity and conservation efforts played a role, he said. ‘‘The best protection is good biosecurity, close monitoring, protecting breeding areas, reducing disturbance and continuing other conservation work like trapping that supports penguin populations.’’ Bird species on DOC’s vaccination programme included kākāpō, takahē, tūturuatu/shore plover, kakī/black stilt and kākāriki karaka/orange-fronted parakeet. Location and population
Kaikōura, New Zealand; little penguins (kororā) with a small and declining population.
Event
Kaikōura’s little penguins were excluded from the Government’s avian flu vaccination list by the Department of Conservation (DOC), as the birds are not considered at risk of extinction and wildlife vaccinations are deemed labor-intensive and stressful.
Scale, impact and risk

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

明尼苏达州又有三处家禽养殖场检测出H5N1病毒 随着秋季候鸟迁徙病毒向南传播 - 医疗日报

Three More Minnesota Poultry Flocks Test Positive for H5N1 as Fall Waterfowl Migration Brings Virus South - Medical Daily
新闻简报随着秋季候鸟迁徙,又有三处明尼苏达州的商业家禽养殖场检测出H5N1病毒。明尼苏达州动物健康委员会于9月10日确认了在比格石县的一处55,701只鸟类养殖场中存在该病毒,此前已于9月1日和9月20日检测到该病毒。这使得明尼苏达州受影响的养殖场总数达到11处,截至2026年,已有约118,000只鸟类受到影响。全国范围内,最近30天内有16处养殖场检测出阳性,总共影响了约400,000只鸟类。风险主要针对与鸟类有直接接触的人群,例如家禽饲养者、水禽猎人以及处理感染禽舍的工作人员。该事件已被确认,没有进一步调查或不确定性的迹象。实际关注点仅限于与鸟类有密切接触的人群,且该情况符合季节性趋势,而非异常事件。
查看新闻五要素
时间
2026年9月20日 上午9:00 EDT
地点与对象
美国明尼苏达州;商业家禽养殖场;家禽饲养者、水禽猎人以及处理感染禽舍的工作人员
事件
明尼苏达州又有三个禽 flock 测试呈 H5N1 禽流感 阳性,明尼苏达州动物健康委员会于 9 月 10 日确认了该病毒在比格石县一家商业肉鸡 flock 中的感染,该 flock 有 55,701 只,且此前两次检测结果于 9 月确认。
规模、影响与风险
截至2026年,H5N1病毒已影响明尼苏达州的11处养殖场,共计约118,000只鸟类。全国范围内,最近30天内有16处养殖场检测出阳性,总共影响了约400,000只鸟类。风险主要针对与鸟类有直接接触的人群。
应对、状态与不确定性
明尼苏达州动物健康委员会确认了家禽养殖场中的H5N1病毒。实际关注点仅限于与鸟类有直接接触的人群,该事件已被确认,未提及进一步调查或不确定性。

Three More Minnesota Poultry Flocks Test Positive for H5N1 as Fall Waterfowl Migration Brings Virus South - Medical Daily

News Brief
Three more commercial poultry flocks in Minnesota tested positive for H5N1 avian influenza as fall waterfowl migration brought the virus south. The Minnesota Board of Animal Health confirmed the virus in a flock of 55,701 birds in Big Stone County on September 10, following earlier detections on September 1 and September 20. This brings the total number of affected operations in Minnesota to 11, with approximately 118,000 birds impacted so far in 2026. Nationally, 16 flocks tested positive over a recent 30-day period, affecting around 400,000 birds. The risk is primarily for individuals with direct contact with birds, such as backyard flock owners, waterfowl hunters, and crews responding to infected barns. The event is confirmed, with no indication of further investigation or uncertainty. The practical concern is limited to those who interact closely with birds, and the situation is consistent with seasonal trends rather than an unusual event.

Conditions Three More Minnesota Poultry Flocks Test Positive for H5N1 as Fall Waterfowl Migration Brings Virus South Published Sep 20, 2026 9:00 AM EDT By Joseph James SAID KHATIB | AFP via Getty Images Share on Twitter Share on Facebook Share on Pocket Minnesota animal health officials have confirmed highly pathogenic H5N1 avian influenza at three more commercial poultry operations, adding about 118,000 birds to the state's fall total during the weeks when ducks and geese begin moving south. The Minnesota Board of Animal Health confirmed the virus in a commercial meat bird flock of 55,701 in Big Stone County on Sept. 15. Two earlier detections, both confirmed Sept. 10, involved 44,352 meat turkeys in Becker County and a commercial breeding operation holding 18,000 birds in Otter Tail County, according to state figures reported in coverage of three more turkey operations hit by bird flu. For the overwhelming majority of Minnesota households, this changes nothing about grocery shopping or holiday meal planning. The practical concern is narrower. It involves the small number of people who will have direct, hands-on contact with birds over the next several weeks: backyard flock owners, waterfowl hunters, and the crews who respond to infected barns. Inside the Latest Minnesota Detections The three new cases follow a quiet summer. Minnesota's previous detection came on May 20, and the virus did not reappear until Sept. 1, in a flock of 29,800 commercial meat turkeys in Wright County, west of Minneapolis. Avian influenza had affected 11 operations in Minnesota so far in 2026, compared with 31 in each of the two previous years, according to figures the Board of Animal Health provided in early September. Nationally, the picture is consistent with a seasonal upswing rather than an unusual event. The Center for Infectious Disease Research and Policy at the University of Minnesota, summarizing U.S. Department of Agriculture postings, reported that 16 flocks tested positive over a recent 30-day window, including 10 commercial and six backyard operations, affecting roughly 400,000 birds in total. Every confirmed detection is posted by the USDA on its commercial and backyard flock detections page. Those bird totals are easy to misread. Federal policy requires depopulating an entire flock once H5N1 is confirmed, whether or not individual birds are sick, which is why a single detection at a large operation produces a number in the tens of thousands. The counts reflect birds culled to stop spread, not a death toll from the disease. Migration Routes Now Carrying the Virus South The timing is not coincidental. Wild bird detections increased this month, and all recent findings were in mallards or other waterfowl, the species most likely to carry avian influenza without appearing sick. Producers in the region watch for exactly this pattern each spring and fall, when migratory birds move through and environmental contamination becomes the most common route into a commercial barn. Officials have not publicly identified how the virus entered each affected Minnesota farm, and that remains unconfirmed. Biosecurity reduces the odds of introduction but cannot eliminate it, which is why detections cluster during migration even at operations with strong protocols. Minnesota raises more turkeys than any other state. That concentration is why the state registers larger bird-loss numbers during a fall wave, and why it maintains its own poultry testing laboratory in Willmar to turn samples around quickly when a grower reports unexplained deaths. Households Closest to the Risk This Fall Human infections remain rare and almost entirely occupational. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention has recorded 71 human A(H5) cases in the United States since February 2024, nearly all in dairy and poultry workers with direct animal contact. The agency says on its bird flu situation page, last updated in March, that "the current public health risk is low" and that it is working with states to monitor people with animal exposures. Its separate surveillance and human monitoring page, updated Sept. 4 with data through Aug. 29, reports that federal flu systems show no indicators of unusual influenza activity in people, including A(H5). No human infection linked to the current Minnesota detections has been publicly reported. The people who should pay attention are those whose hands touch birds. Backyard flock owners should limit contact between their birds and wild waterfowl, keep feed and water covered, clean footwear between locations, and call a veterinarian or the state board about any unexplained cluster of deaths. Hunters cleaning ducks and geese should use gloves and eye protection, avoid handling birds that appeared sick, and wash hands and equipment thoroughly. The Minnesota Department of Natural Resources maintains hunter guidance and reports that all wild turkeys it has sampled have tested negative. Anyone who develops fever, cough, or eye irritation within 10 days of contact with poultry, wild birds, or cattle should call a clinician before going in and mention the exposure. Properly cooked poultry remains safe, and the USDA advises cooking poultry to an internal temperature of 165 degrees Fahrenheit. Pasteurized dairy is safe, while raw milk should be avoided. MedicalDaily previously reported on the earlier September cluster of seven commercial detections across turkey country in the Dakotas, Minnesota and Idaho. What has changed since then is the concentration of new cases inside Minnesota itself and the documented increase in wild waterfowl findings that typically precedes further farm outbreaks. What remains unknown is how far the fall wave will extend, whether additional states will report commercial detections, and how the virus reached each barn. The USDA posts confirmations as laboratories complete them, and the Board of Animal Health updates its own page as Minnesota cases are confirmed. For most readers, the reasonable response is no change in daily routine. For the smaller group with direct bird contact this autumn, protective equipment and prompt reporting of sick birds are the steps that matter, and they matter now rather than in November. Read more Bird Flu Detections Resume in US Dairy Herds and Poultry Flocks After a Month Without Federal Updates Seven Bird Flu Detections Since September 1 Hit Turkey Farms Across the Dakotas, Minnesota and Idaho Scientists Develop Nasal H5N1 Vaccine Designed to Block Bird Flu Where the Virus Enters the Body Key Questions Answered What was confirmed in Minnesota? H5N1 at three more commercial poultry operations: 55,701 birds in Big Stone County confirmed Sept. 15, and 44,352 turkeys in Becker County plus 18,000 breeding birds in Otter Tail County, both confirmed Sept. 10. Why are the bird numbers so large? Federal rules require depopulating an entire flock once the virus is confirmed, so one detection at a large operation accounts for tens of thousands of birds. Is the general public at risk? The CDC assesses the risk to the general public as low. U.S. human cases have been rare and almost entirely limited to workers with direct animal contact. Who should take extra precautions? Backyard flock owners, waterfowl hunters, poultry and dairy workers, and depopulation crews. Gloves and eye protection are recommended when handling birds. Is turkey safe to eat? Yes, when cooked to an internal temperature of 165 degrees Fahrenheit. Federal safeguards make it unlikely that infected poultry enters the food supply. What symptoms should exposed people watch for? Fever, cough, sore throat, muscle aches, or eye redness within 10 days of contact. Call a clinician first and mention the animal exposure. Will this cause a turkey shortage? Federal officials have not indicated that. The confirmed losses represent a small fraction of annual U.S. production. 高致病性禽流感 在博伊恩县多物种庭院鸡群中被检测到 - westerniowatoday.com

Highly Pathogenic Avian Influenza Detected in a Multi-Species Backyard Flock in Boone County - westerniowatoday.com
新闻简报On September 23, 2026, the Iowa Department of Agriculture and Land Stewardship confirmed a case of Highly Pathogenic Avian Influenza (H5N1) in a multi-species backyard flock in Boone County, Iowa. This marks the seventh H5N1 case in the state in 2026. The virus affects both wild and domestic birds, with H5N1 often being fatal to domestic poultry. Officials are advising poultry producers and backyard flock owners to strengthen biosecurity measures to protect their birds. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) has stated that the risk to the general public remains low. Individuals who suspect H5N1 in their flocks are encouraged to contact a veterinarian immediately. The Iowa Department of Natural Resources is also requesting reports of five to 10 or more sick or dead wild birds in a small area. Consumers are assured that eggs and poultry products remain safe when properly handled and cooked.

Weather Current Conditions Weather Radar LATEST Meet The 2026 Nodaway Valley Homecoming CandidatesUpdate on Cedar Rapids Police Department Officer-Involved ShootingAtlantic and Creston Set for Homecoming Showdown as District Play BeginsAtlantic Girls Tennis Team Defeats St. Albert in Red OakThrive Iowa Alliance Meeting Hosting Panel DiscussionIGHSAU Volleyball RankingsGlenwood Police Make Three Drug-Related ArrestsHarlan Cross Country Meet Cancelled for SaturdayCoca-Cola Days Returns This Weekend with New Family ActivitiesRed Oak Council Addresses Levee, Parking and DrainageTwo Hurt in Montgomery County CrashAdditional Rain Could Push Iowa Toward September Rainfall RecordsAtlantic Police Report Several Arrests and CitationsAdair County Supervisors Set Public Hearing Date on Culvert Project Shop Local And Save 63° Mostly Sunny Dew Point: 60° Winds: E @ 6 mph Pressure: 30.2" View forecast > Highly Pathogenic Avian Influenza Detected in a Multi-Species Backyard Flock in Boone County By Tom Robinson Sep 23, 2026 | 3:29 PM (Des Moines) The Iowa Department of Agriculture and Land Stewardship and the U.S. Department of Agriculture have confirmed a case of Highly Pathogenic Avian Influenza (H5N1) in a multi-species backyard flock in Boone County. The detection marks Iowa’s seventh H5N1 case in 2026. The virus affects wild and domestic birds. Wild birds can carry the disease without showing signs of illness, while H5N1 is often fatal to domestic poultry, including chickens and turkeys. State agriculture officials are encouraging Iowa poultry producers and backyard flock owners to strengthen biosecurity practices to protect their birds. Anyone who suspects H5N1 in their flock should contact a veterinarian immediately. Suspected cases must also be reported to the Iowa Department of Agriculture and Land Stewardship at 515-281-5305. Signs of H5N1 in birds may include a sudden increase in deaths, lethargy, reduced appetite, decreased egg production, misshapen or thin-shelled eggs, swelling of the head or other body parts, purple or blue discoloration of the comb, wattles or legs, breathing difficulties, coughing, sneezing, nasal discharge, stumbling, falling and diarrhea. The state says eggs and poultry products remain safe to consume when properly handled and cooked. Consumers should cook eggs and poultry to an internal temperature of 165 degrees Fahrenheit. The Iowa Department of Natural Resources is also asking people who find five to 10 or more sick or dead wild birds in a small area to report their findings to local DNR Wildlife Management staff. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention continues to consider the risk to the general public low. Questions about public health should be directed to the Iowa Department of Health and Human Services. Confirmed H5N1 cases are added to tracking information on the USDA APHIS website following confirmation by the National Veterinary Services Laboratory in Ames.
